        FOREWORD
A PAR T from laying foundations of
demography in tbis subcontinent, a hundred
years of the Indian Census has also produced
'elaborate and scholarly accounts of the varie·
gated phenomena of Indian life-sometimes
with no statistics attached, but usually with
just enough statistics to give empirical under-
pinning to their conclusions.' In a country,
largely illiterate, where statistical or numerical
comprehension of even such a simple thing as
age was liable to be inaccurate, an understand-
ing of the social structure was essential. It
was more necessary to attain a broad under-
standing of what was happening around one..
self than to wrap oneself up in 'statistical
ingenuity' or 'mathematical manipulation.'
This explains why the Indian Census came to
be interested in 'many by-paths' and 'nearly
every branch of scholarship, from anthropo-
logy and sociology to geography and religion.'
   In the last few decades the Census has in-
creasingly turned its efforts to the presentation
of village statistics. This suits the temper of
the times as well as our political and economic
structure. For even as we have a great deal of
centralization on the one hand and'decentrali-
zation on the other, my colleagues though it
would be a welcome continuation of the Cen-
sus tradition to try to invest the dry bones of
village ·statistics with flesh-and·blood accounts
of social structure and social change. It was
accordingly decided to select a few villages in
every State for special study, where personal

                                                      VII!
                                                               PREFACE
     The Reglstrar General's foreword gives a very clear idea of what the
project aims at achieving. For us it was not always very easy to select
villages where such surveys could be carried out. We inevitably collected
material for some of the places which have interested us and after a number
of considerations a selection was made.
     Chergaon interested us because agricuiture and castes. polyandry and
trends are found here which are not common in other parts of Himachal
Pradesh. Then there is growing economy ~with elaborate potentialities. We
wanted to study the changes that have taken place here in the last decade
and a half or more.
     This is not a snobbish reed but a brief monograph on socio-economic
survey of Chergaon about which we have gathered as much a vivid infor-
mation of panorama of village life here as we possibly could. It takes years
to collect our material and to piece these together we have made a number
of initial mistakes. To bring out nearly 36 monographs of village surveys in
some of the remotest 'parts of Himachal Pradesh and to bring out another
large number of books means a considerable effort. patience. co-ordination
and devotion to work.
     If we have been able to achieve this in some measure. what will stand
the test of time it would be because of the considerations that we have
received from Shri Asok Mitra, the Registrar General and from other
colleagues. Speaking of those from our organisation here, the initial
schedules have been very carefully and meticulously prepared by Durga
Singh our Office Superintendent. The Investigator, Mohan La! Gupta has
been to Chergaon many a times and has spent weeks together and much
longer. Some of us have been to· Chergaon several times. L. C. Sharma
and I have attended the Rohru fair at different times.
_    We have very vivid memories of these very beautiful and fascinating
'areas. As years passed this is no D).ore a remote area, the motor road and
'changing trends of agricultural economy are obvious. The soil seems to be
 more hospitable. Right from Simla in October or, in autumn one can see the

                                      IX
                                                                                    PREFACE

Irural way side echoing different prosperity for the present and the future.
There are tonnes of selected potatoes, apples and a bumper paddy crops at
the road side. Then hundreds of trucks go carrying apple. Areas from Rohru
 in Chergaon and from lubbal onwards are very beautiful indeed for their
forests. Jubbal has been a very famous State in the past. All along these
areas some attractions or the other are found during the year.
      Nature is lavish in the different times of year. When the road is ready,
Chergaon can attract probably ~ large number of tourists to visit these areas.
Rohru to Chergaon is an attractive walk. There are several kinds of trees,
Forest Nursery and trout fishing and these conjure for a delightful holiday.
Chergaon has a very comfortable Forest Rest House and is now receiving
more attention. There is a Block Development office which was recently
opened there'. More Government officials are visiting these areas and this is
a way to Dodra Kwar. Eld~ers have a sense of wit and humour they compare
to tlie areas in'which they live with whatever is more sophisticated they call
the Mall and the Lower Bazar o~ Simla. The women go to the fields to work
there with jewellery very often. Th,ey get together to celebrate fairs and
festivals. They are elegantly dressed 'in their own typical dresses and in their
own typical jewellery. We write in great deals the many things that have
interested us-the folk dances. Khushal Chand has been to many parts and
collected information on fairs and festivals and was there for the Bhoj festival.
I must say the photographs reveal an amazing peculiarities. One thing is abso-
 lutely certain that the type of horticulture that is indicating the changes,
would one day bring considerable prosperity to the peasants here. The change
 of social customs and polyandry is there but the people have stuck to their
dresses, the typical and their belief for the village gods and it seems this will
not disappear soon.
      I am grateful to Dr, B. K. Roy Burman, Officer on Special Duty
in the office of the Registrar Gen~ral, India, for going through the draft
 report and his comments.
     Most of the excellent photographs and drawings have been prepa1'led by
O.C. Handa. B.S. Thakur, Dharam Pal Sood, Mohan Lal Gupta and
L.C. Sharma have taken photographs and done arts work. Apart from these,
I have taken some pictures. The cover page photograph is an excellent study
taken of a group of ladies of a well-known family of Rohru Tehsil in their
"typical dresses and ornaments. This study was taken up by O.C. Handa at
the Hatkoti fair and shows very typical ornaments and dresses.

                                             RAM CHANDRA PAL SINGH
BOSWELL SIMLA;
14 Nov. 1965
ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS
      The study would probably not have been
   complete, had the villagers not rendered proper
   assistance to the investigator. We owe special
    thanks to Shiv Saran a witty old mate, who
   introduced the investigator to every villager.
   He gave them an introductory talk about the
   object of our survey work and has a fairly
   wide knowledge of settlement history of
   different castes. Sunder Singh Panchayat Secre~
   tary and Thakur Lila Singh talked about the
   agriculture. Kahan Singh tells all sorts of
   stories about evil spirits and superstitions.
    Gian Chand, weaver of Sonda is a master
    craftsman. Master Parma Nand was kind
    enough to give a number of folk songs.
     Lastly, we are grateful to Sohan Lal, Magis-
   trate 1st class who showed the old records
   for Chergaon village. Devi Saran, the village
   patwari is a fountain of information relating
   to revenue. Hari Ram, B.D.O. of the newly
   created block was helpful to the investigator.
   R. K. Gupta, Tech. Asstt. of the agriculture
   Department has kindly checked up the agri-
   culture portion in Chapter III.
       To all of them, we are extremely grateful.


                RAM CHANDRA PAL SINGH

                                               Climate and Rainfall
                                                 The surroquding hills remain snow-cover-
                                               eo ior more 't'nan six months ':m a -year.
                                               Winter is chilly. $now continues upto
                                               January and February. The average snow-
                                               fall is two and a half feet. The heaviest
                                               snowfall was during 1959-60 winter which
                                               WaS more than six feet. The climate is cold.
                                               Sweeping cold winds blow from the valleys.
                                                 In summer, the- cLimate is wanm~r. The
                                               pleasant seasons are Spring and Autumn.
                                               These are mild and brading. During rains
                                               most of the rain falls during July, August
                                               and September. Little rain also falls during
                                               winter. Average yearly rainfall is 35". 1947
                                               was the worst year when it rained too much.
                                               There were floods in Pabber and Andhra.
   'P\.%~!\)l\~

    Sparrow                                    and low-lying fields on the banks of the
    Quail                                      stream were washed away.

   Trees are obtained by applying to the
Forest Department through the Panchayat.
The quantity of material required is usually
recommended by the Panchayat.
   Labowr-Prior to 1947-48, stones and
wood used to be collected by all villagers
and the owner used to give meals to them.
This was not a forced labour. By this the
work was done' quickly in a collective form.
At the site of the stone quarry or the place
from where timber was brought, the work
was done by the owner at cheap rates. The
masons and carpenters were working on a
contract. The charges for construction are
agreedl, before the work starts. The mason
used to charge Rs. 20 for a one-storeyed
house and Rs. 35 for a double storeyed
house.                                             wood. No mud mortar is used. When the door

dead body, return to their homes except the       house for an emergency in: case anyone dies.
chief mourner and other 4-6 persons.
These persons bathe in the stream before
they return home. When the chief mourner
sees that the body is consumed by the
flames, he touches the deceased's skull with
a long pole. The rite is called kepal kriya or
chopna in their dialect. Then he returns
hoine where he is again consoled by the
gathering. The nearest relatives stay for five
to seven days. H death takes place before
noon, the dah sanskar is performed the same
day and if it takes place in the evening or at
night, the cremation is put off till next
mornjng. AJ watch is kept over the whole
night lest the dead body wakes up and
frightens every one.

  As a rule no food is cooked in the house
where death has taken place. The food for
the family and others are cooked in a sepa-
rate house. The charaj -is sent for after three
days of the death. He purifies the family by
a little puja on the bank of the river. Then
pinds are offered to the deceased soul. These
are made of rice. All cooking on that day is
held on the bank of the river where food is
served. After this ceremony, the family
starts taking fried meals. The clothes of the
deceased are given partly to charaj and
partly to the nath who claims a share.
Next day, the ashes are washed away#into
the stream. Before doing it one of the turi is
allowed to search out Re. 1 and annas four
which were placed in deceased's mouth. In
case of some families, some bones are tied in
a new piece of cloth and kept to be sent to
liardwar for immersion in the Ganges,

ECONOMY                                                                                    39
                RAE! CROPS
   Wheat-This is sown in September-Oc-
tober in kayar or bakhal land. Its chief
operations are watering, two ploughings,
ploughing and sowing. The crop is manured
when it is 3" high and harvested in May-
June. Bundles of crops are carried to houses
and kept for one-two months in tong. These
are gradually threshed, winnowed: and
grains removed.
   Barley-Grown in kayar or bakhal land.
Its operations are watering, one ploughing
and levelling. Wide lines with the help of
plough are ~ade for sowing in October and
November, followed by levelling, the crop is
manured when it is 2-3"high and harvesting
in June. Crops are spread over roofs for
about a 'fortnight and then threshed with the
help of bullocks. Winnowed and grains
stored.
   M asoor-Grown in bakhal land. Plough-
ing, levelling, sowing in 'October; manuring
in Nov.-Dec., and harvested in May.
               KHARIF CROPS
   Paddy-This crop is sown in kayar land
and requires water throughout its opera-
tions. Ffrst of all fields are manured and
watered. After about six ploughings in
March, these are levelled. Paddy (Nursery)
is sown by broadcast method in March-Ap-
ril, again fields are ploughed and planked.
Hoeing when crop is six inches high.
   Now paddy seedlings are to be transplant-
ed to other fields which are prepared as;

Horticulture                                             The Government is taking measure to check
   The-re is a wide scope for the horticulture           further big erosion. By the river sides, the
and the farmers are being attracted towards              cattle are npt permitted to graze. The two
this. The reason is .that a small apple or-              nurseries established at Seema and Cher·
chard around the forest rest·house when in               gaon, would provide different species of trees
full bloom, has tempted the people. Some                 for plantation on the banks of the streams.
apple orchards are        springing    around
Rohru. Government is advancing horti-
cuI ture loan, refundable after five years in
easy instalments. 'The climate and othe·r
soil conditions of this place are SUitable for
horticulture. The best orchai'd which can
flourish here is of apples.
   The fruit plants are collected from Gov-
ernment nurseries at Kotkhai, Khadrala or
Mashobra. Spmetimes, the plants are also
procured from private nurseries of Kotgarh.
Some families have taken horticulture loan
and more villagers are taking. Farmers are
setting aside a part of their holdings for
planting fruit trees. People complain that
cattle destroy their plants in the early
stages. Three families of Thakurs and Raj-
puts have 214 apple plants, which have sur-
vived.
Soil Conservation
  The soil is rocky. There is little' soil con·
servation. Causes of erosion are the destruc·
tion of forests and excessive and uncontrol-
led use of grazing ~astures alon~ the bank~,

 Education
   During 1948, a primary school was opened

   During the day, she has to work in the
fields. She carries cowciung, do~s hoeing,

   In the evening, she cooks food, serves to

kitchen.
   During off days from agriculture, she
~pins wooL After taking food she spins the
wool tiU about 11.00 P.M. Before her husband
and children go to bed, she provides hot
water to clean their feet. She is the last per-
son to go to bed.
Leisure and Recreation
   After crops have, been sown, villagers
find some time for respite. The slack season
are the winter months when it is severely
cold outsid:e.
   Once a while a unit of Himachal Publi-
 city Department gives a play. No dramas are
 staged. In the records of Gram Sewak, it has
 been written down that a dramatic club
 with 20 members was started during 1959.
 But no drama has been staged so far. During
 the course of investigation an effort was
 made by the Government officials and local
 persons to stage a drama, but it did not
finalize.

                                                               v. Conclusion
   Since independence, there had been many        Changes in the relationship of the village
significant changes. What was a remote area     to the world outside also became increasing-
of Bushahr state, is now accessible area and    ly significant after the independence. Shops
developing into a prosperous part of the        and eating houses appeared in the village
Pradesh. There were no means of communi-        where people of different castes mix up
cation it took nearly 6 to 7 days to get to     and talk to each other.
Rohru on a horse or on foot. Now one gets
there in a few hours.                             The change in the people's dress, orna-
   Forced labour-begar, which at one time       ments and manners are observed. The use of
